### [Irritable bowel syndrome and related disorders of gut-brain interaction.](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0140-6736(26)01500-x)

- Source: `lancet_abstracts` · published `2026-10-29` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42956794`

Irritable bowel syndrome, chronic constipation, and functional diarrhoea are disorders of gut-brain interaction, the symptoms of which include abnormal stool consistency or frequency accompanied by varying degrees of abdominal pain, discomfort, bloating, or distension. These conditions affect 20% of the global population with a relapsing and remitting course. Risk factors include female sex, younger age, a history of common mental disorders, and acute enteric infection. The pathophysiology of irritable bowel syndrome, chronic constipation, and functional diarrhoea remain incompletely understood, but are thought to be secondary to aberrant interactions throughout the gut-brain axis, resulting in disordered gut motility and visceral hypersensitivity. These conditions are diagnosed with a combination of clinical history, absence of alarm symptoms-such as weight loss or rectal bleeding-and targeted investigations. An empathetic management approach improves quality of life and symptoms, reduces health-care use, and enhances treatment adherence. Mainstays of treatment include education, dietary and lifestyle advice, modifying fibre intake, and drugs directed towards predominant symptoms. For severe symptoms, other treatments include specific medical devices, biofeedback, or psychological therapies. However, the effectiveness of most treatments is modest and there is an ongoing need to expand available treatments for bowel disorders, with several novel drugs currently in development.

### [Optimal closed-loop feedback control of blood flow after spinal cord injury with norepinephrine](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41551-026-01777-6)

- Source: `nature_biomedical_engineering` · published `2026-10-29` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41551-026-01777-6`

After spinal cord injury, clinical management aims to support tissue perfusion, but current methods rely on the indirect control of arterial blood pressure without direct measurement of the local blood flow. This limits the ability to tailor drug delivery to the injured tissue. Here, we show that spinal cord blood flow can be regulated using a closed-loop controller that adjusts vasopressor infusion on the basis of real-time ultrasound flow measurement. We characterize the dynamic relationship between drug infusion, arterial pressure and spinal cord blood flow, and we use these data to design a feedback controller that targets a predefined flow level. We validate the system in simulations, implement the controller in real time in rodents and translate it to pigs. We show that the controller reduced blood flow deviations and variability compared with constant dosing and supported functional recovery in a rodent survival model. These results show a closed-loop spinal cord perfusion control strategy that leverages clinically available measurements and pharmacological interventions, supporting its translation to clinical settings for the management of acute spinal cord injury. A closed-loop control strategy regulates spinal cord blood flow by adjusting norepinephrine infusion using real-time flow measurements, enabling direct flow targeting after acute injury.

### [Performance of 0/1 h versus 0/3 h pathways in patients with suspected myocardial infarction: a systematic review and individual participant data meta-analysis of randomised controlled trials.](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0140-6736(26)01767-8)

- Source: `lancet_abstracts` · published `2026-10-29` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42956796`

<h4>Background</h4>In patients with suspected myocardial infarction, the 0/1 h pathway (blood draws at 0 h and 1 h) is recommended in current guidelines for early risk stratification. However, individual trials used different safety outcomes, and efficacy outcomes were inconclusive or not assessed. We conducted a systematic review and individual participant data meta-analysis of all randomised controlled trials to synthesise the totality of the evidence, assess heterogeneity across trials, and evaluate clinically relevant outcomes using harmonised definitions across trials.<h4>Methods</h4>In this systematic review and individual participant data meta-analysis, we searched PubMed, Cochrane Central Register of Controlled Trials, Scopus, and the International Clinical Trials Registry Platform from inception to July 6, 2026, without language restrictions, for randomised trials comparing the safety and efficacy of 0/1 h versus 0/3 h pathways (blood draws at 0 h and 3 h, ie, standard care). Individual participant data were obtained and outcome definitions were harmonised. The primary analysis used a two-stage random-effects meta-analysis. After receipt of the individual participant data but before data harmonisation or analysis, the primary safety outcome was refined to a composite of all-cause death or new type 1 myocardial infarction within 30 days, from the originally specified composite of all-cause death or any new myocardial infarction. The primary efficacy outcomes were emergency department length of stay and direct discharge from the emergency department. This meta-analysis is registered with PROSPERO (CRD420261383452).<h4>Findings</h4>Our systematic review identified five randomised trials, all of which provided individual participant data. Among these, the definition of the primary safety outcome varied, and in four trials emergency department length of stay was not reported for the whole analysis population. We included 110 933 patient presentations (55 489 managed with the 0/1 h pathway and 55 444 managed with the 0/3 h pathway). Within 30 days, all-cause death or new type 1 myocardial infarction occurred in 446 patient presentations (0·8%) with the 0/1 h pathway versus 562 patient presentations (1·0%) with the 0/3 h pathway (random-effects pooled odds ratio [OR] 0·83 [95% CI 0·45-1·53]; I<sup>2</sup>=62·9%; τ<sup>2</sup>=0·143). Emergency department length of stay was 314 min (IQR 211-481) in the 0/1 h pathway group versus 308 min (209-453) in the 0/3 h pathway group (random-effects pooled geometric mean ratio 1·03 [95% CI 0·85-1·25]; I<sup>2</sup>=99·2%; τ<sup>2</sup>=0·024). Direct discharge from the emergency department occurred in 37 190 patient presentations (67·0%) with the 0/1 h pathway versus 36 485 patient presentations (65·8%) with the 0/3 h pathway (random-effects pooled OR 1·31 [95% CI 0·79-2·16]; I<sup>2</sup>=97·8%; τ<sup>2</sup>=0·156).<h4>Interpretation</h4>Across the available randomised trials, short-term adverse event rates were low with both pathways, but the pooled safety estimate remained imprecise. On average, earlier cardiac troponin retesting did not reduce emergency department length of stay or clearly increase direct discharge, and substantial between-study heterogeneity indicates that treatment effects might not translate uniformly across health-care systems. For hospitals considering transitioning from a 0/3 h to a 0/1 h pathway, implementation decisions should take broader diagnostic and organisational considerations into account.<h4>Funding</h4>Swiss National Science Foundation, Swiss Heart Foundation, University Hospital Basel, University of Basel, and Foundation for Cardiovascular Research Basel.

### [A single-step injectable Janus-adhesive hydrogel for endoscopic gastrointestinal fistula repair](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41551-026-01820-6)

- Source: `nature_biomedical_engineering` · published `2026-10-30` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41551-026-01820-6`

Gastrointestinal fistulas (GFs) represent one of the main causes of morbidity and mortality. While endoscopic techniques provide a minimally invasive approach to GF repair, offering advantages over conventional surgical approaches, the integration of endoscopic delivery with hydrogel sealants in user-friendly and easy processes approaches remains a great challenge. Here we develop a thermoresponsive positively charged poly(N-isopropylacrylamide) (PNIPAM)/Ga3+ (PGaIII) hydrogel sealant, formed by slug-inspired biomimetic self-assembly. The PGaIII integrated injectability and single-step-formed Janus-type adhesion to tissues, simultaneously featuring excellent biocompatibility, water/acid resistance, active shrinkage and antibacterial properties, enabling its effective ready-to-use biological sealant for endoscopic repair of GFs and prevention of postoperative adhesions. To validate the efficacy of PGaIII in treating GFs, we demonstrate its effectiveness in repairing GFs and its convenience in non-invasive endoscopic surgery in living Sprague–Dawley rat and Bama miniature pig models. The proposed PGaIII holds potential for the repair of fistulas in other organs in vivo, thereby promoting the establishment of safer and more effective clinical treatment options for related diseases. A thermoresponsive hydrogel sealant, prepared in a user-friendly and single-step process, and featuring Janus-type adhesion, is injected endoscopically and repairs gastrointestinal fistulas in rat and minipig models.

### [Admission Mechanisms for Scalable Federated Health Data Collaboration: Design and Proof-of-Concept Study](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e89685)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-10-30` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e89685`

Background: Many digital health and AI initiatives show promising results in pilot settings but do not progress to sustainable services. Pilots can operate within a predefined collaboration, whereas operational services must accommodate changes in participating organizations, available resources, and analytical capabilities while preserving established governance and technical arrangements. Objective: This study examines the pilot-to-service transition as a problem of governed federation evolution and evaluates whether Open Health can admit new forms of participation while preserving bounded capabilities, organizational sovereignty, and auditable decision evidence. Methods: Open Health applies federated computing (FC) as a governance model separated from the analytical runtime. A governed federation context is established before execution, participants receive holder-bound capabilities, and an admission gateway produces explicit ALLOW or DENY decisions before requested operations proceed. The proof of concept uses the Flower federated learning framework and the Pathology Modified National Institute of Standards and Technology (PathMNIST) dataset from the MedMNIST collection with an initial federation comprising hospitals A and B. Mode 1A introduces hospital C as a sponsored contributor permitted to submit locally computed training updates but not invoke the resulting federated model for inference. Mode 1B examines bounded AI-agent participation, where restricted results may be transformed through policy-authorized operations into derivatives whose consumption is governed independently from the source. Evaluation considers admission conformance, signed decision evidence, analytical effects of the additional contribution, and admission overhead. Results: The demonstrator admitted valid member operations and hospital C’s sponsored contribution (mode 1A) while denying model consumption outside hospital C’s capability, scope violations, holder-binding mismatch, and federation-envelope mismatch. Mode 1B extended the same bounded-governance pattern to AI-agent participation, admitting bounded inference and governed transformation but denying unauthorized source consumption and privileged governance access. Under matched training conditions, cancer-related macro recall increased from 50.2% in the A+B control to 67.4% in A+B+C (+17.2 percentage points), while noncancer macro recall changed from 85.5% to 86.3% (+0.8 percentage points). Recall increased from 19.5% to 39.9% for cancer-associated stroma and from 80.9% to 94.9% for colorectal adenocarcinoma epithelium. Median (IQR) full admission latency was 1.210 (1.141-1.333) ms for ALLOW, 1.195 (1.136-1.273) ms for capability-scope DENY, and 0.884 (0.837-0.921) ms for holder-binding DENY. Each evaluated request generated signed decision evidence recording the decision, reason, participant and request bindings, governance context, and related model run. Conclusions: Open Health demonstrates how a federated health-data collaboration can evolve through differentiated participation while preserving membership boundaries and bounded capability relations. The principal contribution is an executable governance boundary preserving capability relations and producing independently verifiable decision evidence separately from the federated-learning runtime. The analytical experiment showed that governed admission of an additional contributor produced a measurable effect on the intended cancer-related classes while preserving noncancer performance. The proof of concept establishes governance behavior under controlled conditions; clinical effectiveness and production-scale performance require further evaluation.

### [Deep Learning–Assisted Diagnosis of Middle Ear Diseases in Otoendoscopy Across Clinician Experience Levels: Retrospective Reader Study](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e96134)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-10-30` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e96134`

Background: Accurate diagnosis of middle ear diseases remains challenging due to the specialized nature of otoscopic interpretation and the global shortage of otolaryngologists. Objective: We aimed to evaluate the impact of a deep learning–based AI support system on the diagnostic performance of clinicians with varying levels of expertise, specifically focusing on its ability to bridge the gap between trainees and experts. Methods: In this retrospective reader study, a deep learning model (EfficientNet-B4) was developed using a large-scale dataset of 7335 endoscopic images. The model was designed for multi-class classification, simultaneously identifying primary categories (chronic otitis media, otitis media with effusion, or none [indicating the absence of the first two]) and secondary features (attic retraction, myringitis, otomycosis, and ventilation tube). To assess clinical impact, diagnostic performance was compared among 3 otology professors, 5 senior residents, and 5 junior residents using an independent clinical assessment set of 100 images. Participants performed diagnoses both with and without AI-generated predictions, with a time constraint of 1 minute per image to simulate real-world clinical pressure. Results: In 5-fold cross-validation, the deep learning model achieved 94.8% accuracy for the primary classification. The stand-alone AI achieved diagnostic accuracies ranging from 94.0% to 99.0% in the clinical assessment set. AI support significantly enhanced diagnostic accuracy across all groups for primary classification, with improvements of 11.0% for otology professors, 28.8% for senior residents, and 32.2% for junior residents (<.001). Accuracy increased from 78.7% to 89.7% for otology professors, from 65.0% to 93.8% for senior residents, and from 54.4% to 86.6% for junior residents. The accuracy gap between the most experienced (otology professors) and least experienced (junior residents) groups was reduced from 24.3% to 3.1%. For secondary categories, significant improvements were observed in diagnosing attic retraction and myringitis among residents. AI-supported accuracy for attic retraction was higher in senior residents than that of the stand-alone AI, although the 95% CIs overlapped substantially, so the difference was not statistically significant. Conclusions: Our findings demonstrate that deep learning–based AI support substantially improves the diagnostic accuracy of middle ear diseases, particularly for less experienced clinicians. By narrowing the performance gap between trainees and experts, this system shows high potential as a valuable adjunct for clinical decision-making and medical education in otology.

### [Domain Knowledge Plus AI Fluency: The New Nonnegotiable Competency for the Health Workforce in the AI Era](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e100954/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-10-30` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e100954/`

In the ongoing AI boom—now supercharged by large language models and generative AI tools—the rapid integration of AI into health care and public health has created an urgent need for professionals who combine deep domain expertise (clinical, epidemiological, ethical, and contextual expertise in health and public health) with AI fluency (the ability to understand, critically evaluate, use, and oversee AI systems effectively). Without domain guidance, AI risks producing biased, unsafe, or contextually irrelevant outputs that compromise patient safety and exacerbate disparities. Conversely, domain experts lacking AI fluency may underuse or misuse these technologies, missing opportunities to improve care quality, operational efficiency, and population health. A foundational understanding of the modern AI system stack—comprising energy, accelerators, infrastructure, models, and applications—empowers health professionals to make informed decisions on adoption, evaluation, governance, and ethical use without needing to code or build models themselves. This stack framework highlights critical interdependencies and health care–specific challenges, including regulatory compliance, data privacy, sustainability, and resource constraints, particularly in low- and middle-income settings. This paper argues that cultivating domain-AI bilingualism is essential for safe, effective, and equitable AI integration worldwide. It outlines the 5-layer AI system stack applied to health care, key reasons for hybrid expertise, and a practical 4-step pathway: awakening (recognizing AI’s transformative role), intrepidity (courageously engaging foundational AI concepts), bilingualism (translating between clinical needs and AI capabilities), and visionary leadership (driving organizational and policy readiness). It outlines major barriers, targeted strategies, and recommendations for educators, institutions, and policymakers. By bridging domain knowledge with AI fluency, health professionals can direct—rather than be directed by—AI technologies, including emerging agentic systems, toward more precise, sustainable, and human-centered outcomes while mitigating risks. This hybrid capability represents a nonnegotiable competency for the health workforce for thriving in the post-2026 AI era. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/c7ff3fe83612dee0b4e3e8b72eb8adc1" />

### [Medicare and Medicaid Programs; Organ Procurement Organizations Conditions for Coverage: Revisions to the Conditions for Coverage, Final Rule (CMS 3409-F)](https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/medicare-medicaid-programs-organ-procurement-organizations-conditions-coverage-revisions-conditions)

- Source: `cms_fact_sheets` · published `2026-10-30` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/medicare-medicaid-programs-organ-procurement-organizations-conditions-coverage-revisions-conditions`

Medicare and Medicaid Programs; Organ Procurement Organizations Conditions for Coverage: Revisions to the Conditions for Coverage, Final Rule (CMS ‑ 3409-F) Overview The Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS) published a final rule that will continue to strengthen federal oversight of Organ Procurement Organizations (OPOs). This final rule expands on the December 2020 final rule that set the framework for performance metrics and created a tiered system to recertify or decertify OPOs based on their performance on both donation and transplant measures. It also clarifies designations to multiple donation service areas (DSAs), recertification processes, procedures for competition, appeals, information management, and other operational details. Background In 2019, President Trump issued Executive Order 13879 “Advancing American Kidney Health,” directing the Secretary to enhance the procurement and utilization of organs available through deceased donation and to establish more transparent, reliable, and enforceable objective metrics for evaluating an OPO’s performance. In response, CMS published the Organ Procurement Organization (OPO) Conditions for Coverage (CfCs) rule in December 2020. In January 2026, CMS published an additional proposed rule revising the OPO CfCs to clarify outstanding procedural questions and to enable OPOs to make more informed decisions to achieve high performance resulting in the successful procurement, distribution, and transplantation of more lifesaving organs (91 FR 4190). This final rule revises the CfCs for OPOs to clarify outstanding procedural questions for recertification and competition. This rule also revises definitions, clarifies the designation requirements for OPOs, clarifies when an OPO’s service area will be open for competition, and updates the appeals process. CMS remains committed to holding all OPOs accountable for their performance and will be moving to decertify tier 3 OPOs as indicated in the 2020 final rule. This final rule does not change the focus on improving donor and transplant volume, as assessed in the outcome measures, nor does it change the tier structure used for OPO recertification and decertification. The outcome measures focus on two rates — the donation rate and the transplantation rate. OPOs that have both DSAs ranking at or above the upper benchmark are placed in tier 1 and eligible for recertification for another four years. OPOs that have DSAs ranking in tier 2 are the next highest performers. Tier 2 OPO DSAs meet or exceed the lower benchmark on both measures but do not rate high enough to reach tier 1 on both measures. OPOs with DSAs that are in tier 2 are also eligible for recertification but must compete to retain their DSAs. Lastly, OPOs with tier 3 DSAs are the lowest performing OPOs with one or both measures below the lower benchmark. OPOs that only have tier 3 DSAs will be decertified and cannot compete for any other open DSA. The OPO final rule complements HHS’ recently released Kidney Care National Plan, a comprehensive strategy to restore competition and choice in dialysis care, reform the nation's organ transplant system, and prevent kidney failure before it begins. The plan identifies where federal action can improve kidney care across the entire spectrum of kidney disease and deliver greater choice and superior outcomes to Americans. Key Provisions for Strengthening Quality, Safety, and Oversight Unsound medical practices The rule defines “unsound medical practices” as failures by OPOs that create an imminent threat to patient health and safety or pose a risk to patients or the public. These practices include, but are not limited to, failures in governance; patient or potential donor evaluation and management; and procurement, allocation, and transport practices and procedures. This gives CMS a clear, enforceable basis for urgent decertification to protect patients and maintain public trust in the system. Adverse Events and Quality Assessment and Performance Improvement (QAPI) The rule removes the examples currently listed in the definition of “adverse event” and adds a revised list to the QAPI requirements. All OPOs must have QAPI programs that use data to monitor and improve their own performance. OPOs are specifically required to have policies for identifying, reporting, analyzing, and preventing adverse events that occur during the organ donation process. This change gives clear information so that all OPOs can act to prevent adverse events, investigate all adverse events, and take appropriate action to prevent future similar events. Pancreata Used for Islet Cell Research The rule clarifies that a donor whose pancreas is procured only for islet cell research counts as a donor for the donation rate measure. It removes pancreata used for islet cell research from the transplant rate measure, so islet cell research activity alone will no longer count as a transplant in the absence of a transplant. These refinements better incentivize both pancreata islet cell research and transplantation while more precisely measuring performance. Documentation of Research Organs This rule requires OPOs to maintain records on the disposition of organs recovered for bona fide research studies. This will foster OPO accountability in the responsible disposition of any organ sent for research, including pancreata that are used for islet cell research. Human Resources The rule requires an OPO’s medical director to hold a license in at least one of the states or territories within one of the OPO’s service areas, or as required by applicable law. This requirement addresses OPOs that operate in more than one DSA. Key Provisions for Incentivizing Fair and Equitable Competition to Increase Organ Donation and Transplantation Nonrenewal of Tier 2 OPO Agreements for Designation The rule clarifies that CMS will not renew an OPO’s agreement for designation to a DSA if the OPO is no longer designated to that DSA. This could occur if an OPO with a tier 2 DSA is unsuccessful in the competition process and fails to retain its DSA. In this situation, the OPO may compete for other DSAs or potentially be assigned a DSA by CMS in certain situations. Decertification The rule identifies circumstances in which CMS may involuntarily end an OPO’s agreement: The OPO no longer meets the requirements for certification, including process performance measures. All of the OPO’s DSAs fall into tier 3 in the final assessment period. An OPO is unsuccessful in competition to retain its tier 2 DSA, is not designated to any DSA, and does not have any performance data at the end of the following recertification cycle. In cases of urgent need, CMS may decertify an OPO immediately. This change clarifies that tier 2 status alone is not a basis for decertification, thereby allowing OPOs with tier 2 DSAs to be recertified. Appeals process The rule codifies a process for discretionary review by the CMS Administrator after a hearing officer issues a decision. Competition and DSA transitions The rule clarifies, without changing the regulation itself, how existing competition selection criteria apply under the tier system for recertification. In addition, it adds a new requirement for incumbent OPOs to cooperate with the successor OPO during a transition. It also requires the successor OPO to submit a transition plan with periodic progress reports to CMS. Together, these requirements support uninterrupted service to patients and donor families during a DSA transition. Requirements for certification The rule removes the requirement that an OPO must have been recertified under Medicare between Jan. 1, 2002, and Dec. 31, 2005. The OPO Final Rule complements the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services and its Health Resources and Services Administration (HRSA) ongoing modernization of the national organ transplant system, including new governance, technology, and oversight improvements, to strengthen safety, transparency, and public trust. CMS’ OPO oversight and HRSA’s Organ Procurement and Transplantation Network modernization advance a common goal: safer procurement and more lifesaving transplants. CMS News and Media Group Catherine Howden, Director Media Inquiries Form 202-690-6145

### [Multidomain Outcomes of a Digital Self-Management Program in Rural Adults With Chronic Conditions: Single-Arm Prospective Interventional Study](https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e95283)

- Source: `jmir_mhealth` · published `2026-10-30` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e95283`

Background: Chronic disease and comorbid mental health problems disproportionately affect rural and underserved populations. Digital self-management programs are potentially scalable, but evaluations often focus on narrow outcomes. Objective: This study examined 12-month longitudinal changes in psychological and multidomain outcomes among Pictou County Patient Empowerment Program participants and explored co-occurring changes and psychological moderators. Methods: This single-arm prospective interventional study enrolled 182 community-dwelling adults with ≥1 chronic condition in Pictou County, Nova Scotia, between October 2023 and August 2025 through community-based outreach. Median age was 60 (IQR 51‐68) years, 155 of 182 (85.2%) participants were women, and the median number of chronic conditions was 3 (IQR 1‐3). The initial 6-month digital intervention delivered brief daily modules targeting physical activity, nutrition, stress regulation, sleep, habit formation, and social connection, followed by a 6-month lower-intensity maintenance period during which monthly group videoconference sessions continued to be offered. Outcomes were assessed at baseline, 6 months, and 12 months. Primary outcomes were illness perceptions (Brief Illness Perception Questionnaire), perceived stress (10-item Perceived Stress Scale [PSS-10]), disease-related worry (10-item Questionnaire on Distress in Cancer Patients), life satisfaction (Satisfaction With Life Scale), and mental well-being (World Health Organization-Five Well-Being Index [WHO-5]). Secondary outcomes assessed the physical, behavioral, and psychosocial domains. Generalized estimating equations estimated population-averaged longitudinal changes, adjusted for age, sex, relationship status, comorbidity burden, and baseline depression or anxiety medication use. Exploratory models examined co-occurring changes and moderation. Tests were 2-sided with =.05. Results: Across outcomes and follow-up assessments, completion ranged from 58.2% (95% CI 51.0%-65.2%; 106/182) to 65.4% (95% CI 58.2%‐71.9%; 119/182). At 12 months, illness perceptions decreased (β=−7.3, 95% CI −9.7 to −4.9), perceived stress decreased (β=−2.4, 95% CI −3.5 to −1.3), disease-related worry decreased (β=−6.3, 95% CI −7.8 to −4.7), life satisfaction increased (β=2.5, 95% CI 1.4‐3.5), and mental well-being increased (β=10.4, 95% CI 7.2‐13.5; all <.001). Secondary outcomes improved across fatigue, sleep, diet quality, sedentary behavior, spiritual well-being, mental health–related quality of life, weight, and BMI. Exploratory analyses showed that improvements in fatigue, sleep quality, and illness perceptions co-occurred with greater well-being and life satisfaction and lower perceived stress and disease-related worry at both follow-up assessment points. Clinically meaningful stress improvement (≥4-point PSS-10 reduction) was achieved by 36.4% (95% CI 27.3%-45.6%, 39/107) and 43.4% (95% CI 34.0%-52.8%, 46/106) participants at 6 and 12 months, respectively. At 12 months, 43.5% (95% CI 34.2%-52.9%, 47/108) participants reported clinically meaningful improvement in mental well-being (≥10-point WHO-5 increase). Conclusions: Participation in this low-bandwidth, home-based digital self-management program was associated with sustained psychological, physical, and behavioral improvements among rural adults with chronic conditions. These multidomain findings support randomized controlled evaluation of scalable digital self-management approaches as adjuncts to rural chronic disease care. Trial Registration: ClinicalTrials.gov NCT06064110; https://clinicaltrials.gov/study/NCT06064110

### [Plan Year 2027 Marketplace Plans and Prices Fact Sheet](https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/plan-year-2027-marketplace-plans-prices-fact-sheet-0)

Plan Year 2027 Marketplace Plans and Prices Fact Sheet The Open Enrollment Period to choose Healthcare.gov coverage for 2027 begins on November 1, 2026, and runs through January 15, 2027. In the upcoming year, Americans using HealthCare.gov will have robust access to low-cost plans. President Trump has delivered the largest decrease in Americans’ prescription drug prices in nearly 70 years; made historic investments to expand healthcare access in rural communities; and maximized price transparency. The Trump Administration continues to fight to lower insurance premiums and send money directly to the American people—not big insurance companies. Robust Access to Low-Cost Plans: More than 75% of Americans who purchase coverage through HealthCare.gov will continue to have access to premiums at or below $50 in 2027. The millions of Americans eligible for tax credits will have access to a plan with nearly 90% of the lowest-cost premium paid for by the federal government, compared to 85% in 2020—the last year not impacted by temporary COVID-19 policies. Building off the Historic Health Savings Account (HSA) Eligibility Expansion: Thanks to President Trump’s Working Families Tax Cuts, every American purchasing healthcare through HealthCare.gov has access to HSA-eligible plans. HSAs allow American families to put more money back into their pockets by cutting taxes on healthcare while giving people, not insurance companies, greater control over their health spending. In 2027, eligible enrollees will have access to 35 different health plans on average that are HSA-eligible, a more than 1,000% increase over the status quo prior to President Trump’s historic Working Families Tax Cuts. Robust Choice: In 2027, families using HealthCare.gov will have access to a broad selection of plans to choose from. On average, Americans have access to 100 different plans and five to six different insurance companies. Low-Cost Catastrophic Plans: Catastrophic plans offer access to co-pay-free preventive care and protections for individuals with pre-existing conditions at lower costs. The average premium of the lowest-cost catastrophic plan is nearly 10% cheaper than the next lowest-cost plan at other coverage levels. The Trump Administration will continue to fight to lower drug prices and insurance premiums, hold big insurance companies accountable, and maximize price transparency. To learn more, visit whitehouse.gov/greathealthcare . CMS News and Media Group Catherine Howden, Director Media Inquiries Form 202-690-6145

### [Real-World Functional Gait Recovery Trajectories After Total Hip and Total Knee Arthroplasty via Smartphone Sensors: Observational Longitudinal Study](https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e97657)

- Source: `jmir_mhealth` · published `2026-10-30` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e97657`

Background: Recovery after total hip arthroplasty (THA) and total knee arthroplasty (TKA) is typically assessed with infrequent clinic-based evaluations that may not reflect patients’ everyday mobility. Smartphone inertial sensors enable scalable, repeated gait measurement in real-world settings, but the extent to which prompted walking tests reflect daily life walking during recovery is not well defined. Objective: The objective of this study was to characterize week-by-week recovery trajectories after THA and TKA using high-frequency smartphone sensor data and quantify the difference between prompted in-app walking tests and passive daily life background walking. Methods: We analyzed longitudinal smartphone-based gait measurements from 735 patients (THA, n=350; TKA, n=385) who underwent unilateral surgery between July 2022 and October 2025. Nine spatiotemporal gait parameters were monitored over the first 15 postoperative weeks across 2 conditions: prompted “in-app” walks and passively captured “background” walks. Recovery trajectories were evaluated using Kaplan-Meier time-to-baseline analyses, linear mixed-effects models, and comparison of late-stage plateau levels (weeks 11‐15) using estimated marginal means of the models. Results: Across all parameters, THA patients returned to preoperative baseline levels faster than TKA patients, with mean trajectories generally approaching baseline by week 4 for THA versus weeks 6 to 8 for TKA. Linear mixed-effects models showed that surgical procedure type significantly influenced recovery trajectory for all parameters except single support of the unaffected leg. All gait parameters except stance asymmetry and single support of the unaffected leg exhibited persistent differences through the late-recovery plateau (weeks 11‐15). We also compared prompted “in-app” walks with passively captured background walking. Prompted and passive walking showed systematic divergence in both THA and TKA; prompted assessments generally indicated faster or more complete return to baseline than passive daily life walking. Finally, preoperative gait level was strongly associated with postoperative trajectories, with baseline-stratified analyses showing distinct patterns of recovery between groups. Conclusions: High-frequency smartphone gait assessment quantifies procedure-specific recovery dynamics and demonstrates that prompted tests and passive daily life measurements capture distinct, noninterchangeable functional signals. Parameters from prompted walks plateaued while those from passively captured walks were still changing; prompted testing alone therefore captures only one component of returning function, highlighting the additional information that passive, real-world gait monitoring can provide.

### [Rethinking Health Care Regulatory Sandboxes Under the European Union AI Act](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e98653/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-10-30` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e98653/`

Artificial intelligence (AI) models have enabled substantial advances across a wide range of healthcare applications. Since the entry into force of the Artificial Intelligence Act (EU AI Act) in 2024, these systems have become subject to a common regulatory framework in the European Union intended to harmonize AI governance across all Member States. One of the Act’s distinctive features is that it accommodates existing economic, technological, and regulatory differences among European countries by leaving certain implementation responsibilities to national authorities. This is evident in regulatory sandboxes, supervised environments for developing, testing, and validating AI systems before deployment. In this viewpoint, we propose recommendations for the design of healthcare regulatory sandboxes. These recommendations address the need to document model compression techniques that may affect the classification of large high-risk systems above the 10²⁵ FLOPs threshold, to incorporate explainability into AI systems from the design stage, to identify and document biases in healthcare datasets, to clarify the treatment of federated learning parameter updates under the GDPR, and to strengthen the oversight of AI agents in clinical environments. The implementation timeline makes this discussion particularly timely, given the approaching deadline of 2 August 2026, by which all Member States must have established at least one national regulatory sandbox <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/06dfe37a62557617c9f8483e848c81aa" />

### [The Mediating Role of Sleep Quality in Associations of Smartphone Addiction and Time-Specific Use With Prenatal Depression and Anxiety: Cross-Sectional Observational Study](https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e100064)

- Source: `jmir_mhealth` · published `2026-10-30` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e100064`

Background: Prenatal depression and anxiety have emerged as pressing global public health challenges. In the digital era, excessive smartphone use may exert complex effects on maternal mental health. Prior research has predominantly focused on adolescents, whereas evidence among pregnant women, particularly regarding total weekly use and use during critical circadian windows, such as after waking and before bedtime, remains limited. Furthermore, the mediating role of sleep quality in these associations has yet to be systematically examined. Objective: This study aimed to investigate the associations of smartphone addiction and time-specific smartphone use with prenatal depression and anxiety, and to assess the mediating role of sleep quality in these associations. Methods: This cross-sectional observational study enrolled 537 pregnant women. Smartphone use characteristics, sleep quality, and prenatal depression and anxiety were assessed using the Smartphone Addiction Scale-Short Version (SAS-SV), objective total weekly smartphone use recorded by the device, and self-reported duration of smartphone use after waking and duration of bedtime smartphone use; the Pittsburgh Sleep Quality Index (PSQI), the Patient Health Questionnaire-9 (PHQ-9), and the Generalized Anxiety Disorder-7 (GAD-7), respectively. Binary logistic regression was used to estimate associations, restricted cubic spline (RCS) models were used to explore nonlinear patterns, and exploratory mediation analyses were used to examine whether sleep quality statistically accounted for part of the observed associations. Results: The prevalence of moderate-to-severe prenatal depression and anxiety was 8.8% (47/537) and 5.8% (31/537), respectively. After adjusting for covariates, smartphone addiction was associated with higher odds of depression (odds ratio [OR] 3.461, 95% CI 1.771‐7.100) and anxiety (OR 7.738, 95% CI 3.064‐23.172). Duration of smartphone use after waking ≥61 minutes and duration of bedtime smartphone use ≥91 minutes were also associated with higher odds of affective disorders. RCS analyses further revealed distinct nonlinear patterns across different exposure dimensions: duration of smartphone use after waking displayed U-shaped and J-shaped associations with depression and anxiety, respectively, while total weekly use displayed a U-shaped association with anxiety. Exploratory mediation analyses showed that the indirect pathways through sleep quality were statistically significant for the associations of bedtime smartphone use, weekly smartphone use, and smartphone addiction with prenatal depression, with mediation proportions of 44.89% (0.0065/0.0145), 31.30% (0.0056/0.0183), and 31.48% (0.0415/0.1315), respectively. The corresponding proportions in the anxiety models were 35.83% (0.0044/0.0123), 27.05% (0.0039/0.0144), and 22.73% (0.0271/0.1187). Conclusions: Smartphone addiction and excessive smartphone use after waking and before bedtime were associated with higher odds of prenatal depression and anxiety. Sleep quality may statistically account for part of these associations, but longitudinal studies are needed to establish temporal ordering and causal pathways.

### [Trust, Risk, and Uncertainty Surrounding the Use of AI in Health Care: Scoping Review of Studies on Public Perception](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e100747/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-10-30` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e100747/`

Background: While AI has existed for decades, the introduction of generative AI tools and subsequent advancements in their capability and public use resulted in a shift in public awareness and perceptions of AI. In response, the research community has grown increasingly interested in understanding public perceptions of AI use in various contexts, including health care. Public perceptions shape AI acceptance and consequently integration into health care. It is thus crucial to understand public trust in AI and perceptions of risk as we consider its implementation moving forward. Objective: This study aimed to (1) document the extent of what is known within the literature regarding public perceptions of AI applications in health; (2) interrogate existing definitions and theories of trust being used to guide investigations of public trust in AI; and (3) identify practice and policy considerations and support the design, deployment, and regulation of these technologies in a manner that is trustworthy. Methods: The Arksey and O’Malley scoping review framework and the PRISMA-ScR (Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic Reviews and Meta-Analyses extension for Scoping Reviews) guidelines were used to review publications between 2022 and 2024 across 4 databases, resulting in the inclusion of 41 papers for review. Key findings were then charted and organized into tables to help answer the proposed research questions. Results: Public trust in AI in health care was shaped by discourse relating to AI performance and concerns related to depersonalization, loss of human connection, privacy, bias, and transparency. Trust was found to be higher when AI was supporting rather than replacing health care professionals and was influenced by emotional responses, anthropomorphism, demographic factors, and institutional trust. Furthermore, trust extended beyond algorithms to software interfaces, providers, developers, and governing institutions, highlighting the importance of governance and oversight. Conceptualizations of trust varied widely, and trust was defined through vulnerability, competence, benevolence, and integrity. Different theories identified suggested that trust operates across individual, social, institutional, and technological levels. Finally, trust was closely linked to risk and uncertainty, where perceived risk reduced acceptance, while AI literacy, transparency, and explainability mitigated concerns and fostered trust. Conclusions: Trust in AI is not uniform, and as such, efforts to build and measure trust must account for these nuances to effectively mitigate risk and support informed, ethical AI integration and deployment. On the basis of the findings of this review, we propose a multilevel conceptual framework that provides a general understanding of public trust in AI within a health care context that may guide research and policy and practice considerations. Our findings highlight the importance of considering public trust when implementing AI in health care and may support AI communication strategies, policy development, and trustworthy AI development. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/4d50b199ddb5a5d9bfcb843d4463d945" />

### [Generation of synthetic clinical text: a systematic review](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03438-z)

- Source: `npj_digital_medicine` · published `2026-10-31` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03438-z`

Generating medical synthetic text represents an effective solution for clinical NLP issues like sparsity and privacy. This paper conducts a systematic review on synthetic clinical text generation by formulating quantitative analysis to three research questions concerning the generation’s purpose, generation techniques, and the evaluation methods. We searched four databases including PubMed, Scopus, ScienceDirect and Web of Science for publications associated with the topic and identified 87 relevant articles out of 1148. Results showed that the generation purposes were dedicated to Trustworthy, Medical Assistance, and Language Resource themes. Prompting LLMs was the main source of synthetic text (40 articles). GPTs were the main predominant technique used to generate the text (63 articles), especially ChatGPT (47 articles). Additionally, there were four main aspects of evaluation, including similarity, privacy, structure, and utility, where utility was the prevailing method for assessment (52 articles). The synthetic medical text empowered data augmentation and clinical documentation. Using text alteration, controlling real text portions, and federated learning, generative models can produce privacy-preserving medical text. This accelerates the development of new digital health applications.

### [Understanding clinical reasoning variability in medical large language models: a mechanistic interpretability study](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03423-6)

- Source: `npj_digital_medicine` · published `2026-10-31` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03423-6`

Medical large language models achieving high benchmark accuracy exhibit unexplained variability in clinical tasks, producing errors that clinicians cannot safeguard against. We evaluated clinical reasoning stability in GPT-5, MedGemma-27B-Text-IT, and OpenBioLLM-Llama3-70B using 3035 systematic perturbations from physician-validated oncology SOAP, brief vignette, and MIMIC cases. We trained sparse autoencoders on 1 billion tokens from 50,000 MIMIC-IV clinical notes to decompose their internal representation. We find models exhibit dramatic reasoning instability, shifting staging accuracy by over 50% based solely on prompt format, or generating definitive staging in clinically insufficient scenarios. Sparse autoencoder analysis revealed hierarchical encoding in MedGemma, where high-magnitude features encode lexical identity and low-magnitude features encode contextual meaning. OpenBioLLM distributes information uniformly. We demonstrate that these internal encoding structures differentially affect retrieval and reasoning interventions, suggesting interventions effective for one architecture may harm another. These results motivate architecture-specific safety validation as a component of pre-deployment evaluation for medical LLMs.

### [Adoption of Adjunctive Azithromycin for Unscheduled Cesarean Delivery and Postpartum Infections.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.15010)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-11-01` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `new` · identity: `pmid:42734953`

<h4>Importance</h4>Cesarean delivery is the most common surgery in the US and the most significant risk factor for postpartum infection. Although trial evidence demonstrated that adjunctive azithromycin use at the time of birth reduces postpartum infection in pregnant persons who undergo an unscheduled cesarean delivery, use of azithromycin outside that trial has not been reported.<h4>Objective</h4>To determine the adoption of trial evidence in clinical practice and describe postpartum infection trends among US pregnant persons undergoing unscheduled cesarean delivery.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>Data from Epic Cosmos were used to conduct a difference-in-differences analysis including pregnant individuals who received prenatal care, labored, and gave birth to a liveborn singleton infant at 24 to 43 weeks' gestation from 2013 to 2024. Cesarean birth was considered the treatment group and vaginal birth was considered the control group. The analysis was conducted from July 2025 to June 2026.<h4>Exposure</h4>Relative to the publication of trial evidence in 2016, births that occurred from January 1, 2013, to September 28, 2016, were compared with births that occurred from January 1, 2017, to December 31, 2024.<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>The primary outcomes were perioperative administration of azithromycin and postpartum infection within 6 weeks of delivery. Patient-level linear regression was used to obtain the difference-in-differences estimate.<h4>Results</h4>Of the 1 663 441 infant deliveries included in the analysis, there were 202 234 cesarean births and 1 461 207 vaginal births (mean age of pregnant individuals, 29.3 [SD, 5.7] years; median body mass index at time of delivery, 31.8 [IQR, 28.2-36.5]). From January 1, 2013, to September 28, 2016, azithromycin was administered during 2.2% of cesarean births (721/32 970) vs 0.01% of vaginal births (15/261 624) compared with 39.6% (67 094/169 264) vs 0.04% (421/1 199 583), respectively, from January 1, 2017, to December 31, 2024 (adjusted difference-in-differences estimate, 37.6 percentage points [95% CI, 33.1 to 42.2]). From January 1, 2013, to September 28, 2016, postpartum infection occurred after 9.2% of cesarean births (3048/32 970) vs 2.0% of vaginal births (5311/261 624) compared with 8.0% (13 555/169 264) vs 2.7% (32 581/1 199 583), respectively, from January 1, 2017, to December 31, 2024 (adjusted difference-in-differences estimate, -2.0 percentage points [95% CI, -2.6 to -1.4]).<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>After publication of the trial evidence in 2016, azithromycin use for pregnant persons undergoing unscheduled cesarean delivery increased and postpartum infection rates decreased in the US.

### [Coronary CT Angiography versus Intravascular Ultrasound Guidance for PCI.](https://doi.org/10.1056/nejmoa2610771)

- Source: `nejm_abstracts` · published `2026-11-01` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42969897`

<h4>Background</h4>Intravascular ultrasound (IVUS)-guided percutaneous coronary intervention (PCI) improves clinical outcomes. Coronary computed tomographic angiography (CCTA) provides anatomical and physiological information before cardiac catheterization that may enable PCI planning. Data regarding the use of CCTA-guided PCI as compared with IVUS-guided PCI with respect to clinical outcomes are lacking.<h4>Methods</h4>In this investigator-initiated, international, open-label, randomized, noninferiority trial with blinded end-point adjudication, we randomly assigned patients with a clinical indication for revascularization, at least one epicardial lesion with a diameter stenosis of more than 70%, and a fractional flow reserve derived from CCTA of 0.80 or less in a 1:1 ratio to a CCTA-guided or IVUS-guided PCI strategy. The primary end point was target-vessel failure, defined as a composite of death from cardiac causes, target-vessel myocardial infarction, or ischemia-driven target-vessel revascularization, at 12 months. The noninferiority margin for the absolute risk difference was 4.5 percentage points, with a prespecified hierarchical testing strategy for superiority.<h4>Results</h4>A total of 1105 patients underwent randomization: 556 to the CCTA-guided PCI group and 549 to the IVUS-guided PCI group. The median age of the patients was 65 years, and 21.7% were women. At 12 months, target-vessel failure had occurred in 23 patients (4.1%) in the CCTA-guided PCI group and in 25 patients (4.6%) in the IVUS-guided PCI group (risk difference, -0.42 percentage points; 95% confidence interval, -2.82 to 1.99; P<0.001 for noninferiority; P = 0.73 for superiority). A total of 6 patients in the CCTA-guided PCI group and 9 patients in the IVUS-guided PCI group had clinical procedural complications.<h4>Conclusions</h4>A CCTA-guided PCI strategy was noninferior to IVUS-guided PCI with respect to target-vessel failure at 12 months. (Funded by GE HealthCare and Heartflow; P4 ClinicalTrials.gov number, NCT05253677.).

### [Digital Outreach to Improve Statin Refills in Patients With Low Statin Adherence: The ADHERE-ASCVD Randomized Clinical Trial.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.16372)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-11-01` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `new` · identity: `pmid:42667613`

<h4>Importance</h4>Statin nonadherence is a leading, addressable driver of preventable cardiovascular events. Although health systems routinely use outreach to promote preventive care, most strategies are static and lack evidence from randomized clinical trials.<h4>Objective</h4>To determine whether an adaptive, sequential digital outreach strategy improves short-term statin refill vs usual communication among adults with established atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease (ASCVD) or high ASCVD risk and recent nonadherence within a rapidly implemented, health system-embedded randomized clinical trial.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>Pragmatic, health system-embedded, prospective randomized open blinded end point (PROBE) clinical trial using a 2-stage sequential, multiple assignment, randomized trial (SMART) design. The trial was conducted via Kaiser Permanente Northern California, an integrated health care delivery system serving more than 4.6 million members. Eligible participants were adults 18 years or older with established ASCVD or high ASCVD risk, recent statin nonadherence, and eligibility for all study communication modalities.<h4>Interventions</h4>Participants were randomized 1:1:1:1 to receive secure portal messaging, SMS/text messaging, nonsecure email, or usual communication. Initial nonresponders within 14 days underwent prespecified second-stage randomization to repeat the same outreach, switch communication modality, or receive usual communication.<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>Rates of 14-day statin refill following each randomization, with 28-day follow-up.<h4>Results</h4>Among 20 604 participants (mean [SD] age, 54.8 [9.0] years; 40.9% female; 27.8% Asian, 10.9% Black, 33.6% Hispanic; 15.3% with established ASCVD), initial digital outreach increased 14-day statin refill vs usual communication (14.4% vs 12.0%; adjusted risk difference, 2.3 percentage points [95% CI, 1.3-3.4]; adjusted risk ratio, 1.20 [95% CI, 1.10-1.30]). Among initial nonresponders, second-stage outreach further increased refill (13.0% vs 10.4%; adjusted risk difference, 2.5 percentage points [95% CI, 1.3-3.7]; adjusted risk ratio, 1.25 [95% CI, 1.11-1.39]). Switching communication modalities did not improve refill vs repeating the same modality (13.2% vs 12.5%; adjusted risk ratio, 1.06 [95% CI, 0.94-1.17]). Cumulative statin refill through 28 days was higher in those receiving any digital outreach vs usual communication (24.9% vs 21.2%; adjusted hazard ratio, 1.21 [95% CI, 1.13-1.30]). Results were consistent across prespecified subgroups.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>Among adults with a statin indication and recent statin nonadherence, adaptive outreach improved short-term refill vs usual communication. A second outreach produced additional gains among initial nonresponders, although switching communication modalities provided no additional benefit. These findings support adaptive, sequential health system outreach strategies to improve cardiovascular prevention when integrated into population health programs.<h4>Trial registration</h4>ClinicalTrials.gov Identifier: NCT07522645.

### [Tenecteplase Before Thrombectomy at 4.5 to 24 Hours for Basilar Artery Occlusion: The ATTENTION LATE Randomized Clinical Trial.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.15496)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-11-01` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `new` · identity: `pmid:42776543`

<h4>Importance</h4>Whether intravenous tenecteplase before endovascular thrombectomy (EVT) improves outcomes in patients with basilar artery occlusion presenting 4.5 to 24 hours after stroke onset remains uncertain.<h4>Objective</h4>To evaluate the efficacy and safety of intravenous tenecteplase before EVT vs EVT alone in patients with moderate to severe basilar artery occlusion admitted within 4.5 to 24 hours from stroke onset.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>This multicenter, open-label, blinded end point, randomized clinical trial was conducted at 40 stroke centers in China. Between March 16, 2023, and February 4, 2025, a total of 330 patients with moderate to severe basilar artery occlusion (National Institutes of Health Stroke Scale score ≥10) presenting within 4.5 to 24 hours from stroke onset to EVT-capable centers were enrolled. Final follow-up was completed on May 4, 2025.<h4>Interventions</h4>Patients were randomized 1:1 via a centralized web server using a minimization method to receive either intravenous tenecteplase (bolus injection, 0.25 mg/kg; maximum, 25 mg) followed by EVT (n = 166) or EVT alone (n = 164).<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>The primary outcome was functional independence (modified Rankin Scale score, 0-2) at 90 days. Safety outcomes included symptomatic intracranial hemorrhage and 90-day mortality.<h4>Results</h4>Among 332 patients randomized, 2 withdrew consent. Of the remaining 330 patients (mean [SD] age, 66 [11] years; 241 males [73%]; 89 females [27%]), 329 completed 90-day follow-up. Of whom, 50 of 165 (30%) in the tenecteplase plus EVT group and 50 of 164 (30%) in the EVT alone group achieved functional independence (adjusted rate ratio, 0.92 [95% CI, 0.67 to 1.25]; risk difference, -0.18% [95% CI, -10.13% to 9.76%]). Symptomatic intracranial hemorrhage occurred in 8 patients (5%) in the tenecteplase plus EVT group and 6 (4%) in the EVT alone group. Mortality at 90 days was similar between the groups (66 [40%] vs 70 [43%], respectively).<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>Among patients with moderate to severe basilar artery occlusion admitted directly to EVT-capable centers who presented 4.5 to 24 hours after stroke onset, the addition of intravenous tenecteplase before EVT did not result in improved functional outcomes compared with EVT alone.<h4>Trial registration</h4>ClinicalTrials.gov Identifier: NCT05701956.

### [Abbott starts pivotal trial for mitral valve replacement device](https://www.medtechdive.com/news/abbott-starts-pivotal-trial-for-mitral-valve-replacement-device/873409/)

- Source: `medtech_dive_primary` · published `2026-11-02` · freshness `fresh`

- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.medtechdive.com/news/abbott-starts-pivotal-trial-for-mitral-valve-replacement-device/873409/`

Abbott has started a pivotal clinical trial of its investigational Cephea mitral valve system, the company said Thursday. The device, which Abbott acquired in its 2019 takeover of Cephea Valve Technologies, replaces the diseased mitral valve to restore normal blood flow through the heart. Cephea performs the same function as Abbott’s Tendyne but is delivered through a vein in the leg, rather than via a small incision in the chest.

### [Antithrombotic Therapy After Left Atrial Appendage Occlusion: The SIMPLAAFY Randomized Clinical Trial.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.21361)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-11-02` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `new` · identity: `pmid:42970643`

<h4>Importance</h4>Following a left atrial appendage closure (LAAC) procedure, antithrombotic therapy is administered to safely allow time for device endothelialization. However, the optimal postimplant antithrombotic regimen is unknown.<h4>Objective</h4>To determine if either reduced-dose non-vitamin K oral anticoagulants (NOACs) or aspirin alone are noninferior to dual antiplatelet therapy (DAPT) for the composite clinical end point of death, stroke, systemic embolism, or major bleeding through 6 months post LAAC.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>This open-label, 3-group, multicenter clinical trial randomized 1857 patients between October 2024 and December 2025 at 87 US investigational sites, with the final 6-month follow-up in July 2026. Enrolled patients had nonvalvular atrial fibrillation, underwent successful LAAC, and were eligible for all 3 regimens.<h4>Interventions</h4>Patients were randomly allocated 1:1:1 to DAPT for 6 months followed by aspirin (n = 624), a reduced-dose NOAC for 3 months followed by aspirin (n = 620), or aspirin alone for 12 months (n = 613).<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>The primary outcome was the 6-month composite of all-cause death, stroke, systemic embolism, or major bleeding. Noninferiority was tested in the primary analysis population with a 1-sided Farrington-Manning test (α = .025). Bleeding and 45-day device-related thrombus rates were exploratory without multiplicity adjustment.<h4>Results</h4>The cohort had a mean age of 76 (SD, 8) years, 42.9% were female, the mean CHA2DS2-VASc score was 4.2 (SD, 1.3), and 68.0% had paroxysmal atrial fibrillation. The primary outcome occurred in 41 patients (6.9%) with DAPT vs 31 (5.1%) with a reduced-dose NOAC (risk ratio, 0.75 [95% CI, 0.48-1.18]; risk difference, -1.7 percentage points; 97.5% upper bound, 1.2) and 20 (3.3%) with aspirin alone (risk ratio, 0.49 [95% CI, 0.29-0.82]; risk difference, -3.5 percentage points; 97.5% upper bound, -0.8). Both monotherapy strategies met noninferiority criteria (P < .001 for noninferiority for both). Major bleeding was 4.5% with DAPT, 2.7% with reduced-dose NOACs, and 1.7% with aspirin alone; clinically relevant nonmajor bleeding was 8.7%, 4.8%, and 3.8%, respectively. Device-related thrombus was observed in 3.4%, 0.9%, and 2.7%, respectively.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>Both reduced-dose NOACs and aspirin alone were noninferior to DAPT for 6-month outcomes after uncomplicated LAAC.<h4>Trial registration</h4>ClinicalTrials.gov Identifier: NCT06521463.

### [Context-Dependent Clinical Narrative Signals of Renal Function Deterioration in Cross-Language Settings: Retrospective Multicohort Study](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e94249)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-11-02` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e94249`

Background: Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes (KDIGO) classifies chronic kidney disease (CKD) using categories based on both glomerular filtration rate and albuminuria. Structured measures remain essential but may not capture contextual clinical concerns documented in narrative records. Objective: This study examined whether text-derived clinical-status language features in clinician notes were associated with deterioration in renal function in independent Greek- and English-language clinical cohorts. Methods: We retrospectively analyzed a Greek general-nephrology cohort (314 patients; 10,306 estimated glomerular filtration rate [eGFR] measurements) and an independent English-language Medical Information Mart for Intensive Care IV (MIMIC-IV) replication cohort. The Greek primary analysis used a linear mixed effects model over a follow-up period of up to 1797 days. The MIMIC-IV primary analysis used a 90-day, patient-level fixed-horizon competing-outcome model (N=87,533). Separate analyses of Greek and MIMIC-IV data evaluated discrimination, calibration, and sensitivity to alternative feature specifications. Results: In the Greek cohort, a 1-SD higher negativity was associated with lower eGFR (=−3.273 mL/min/1.73 m², 95% CI −5.049 to −1.498; <.001) and a more negative time trajectory (time-by-score =−1.324, 95% CI −2.553 to −0.096; =.035). In MIMIC-IV, a 1-SD higher negative-vs-reassuring polarity contrast was associated with renal deterioration (relative risk ratio [RRR]=1.090, 95% CI 1.070‐1.110; <.001). Within eGFR G categories, polarity-score areas under the curve (AUCs) were 0.506 to 0.552 using discharge eGFR and 0.518 to 0.534 using the worst observed eGFR. Discrimination for renal deterioration remained modest (one-vs-rest AUC=0.6134, 95% CI 0.6092‐0.6175), and adding both natural language processing (NLP) features to the clinical model increased AUC by only 0.0018 (95% CI 0.0010‐0.0025). Conclusions: Independently constructed clinical-language measures were associated with renal function deterioration across two languages and health care settings. The findings support cross-language replication of an association signal, not formal external validation, measurement equivalence, or clinically useful stand-alone prediction.

### [Diagnosing Rare Genetic Diseases From Facial Images Using Visual Prompting With Multimodal Large Language Models: Diagnostic Accuracy Study](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e105860)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-11-02` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e105860`

<strong>Background:</strong> Rare genetic diseases often present with recognizable facial phenotypes. Whether multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can prioritize the diagnosis of rare genetic diseases from facial images and whether image enhancement or visual prompting can improve this process remain unclear. <strong>Objective:</strong> This study aimed to evaluate the diagnostic accuracy of MLLMs for rare genetic diseases using facial images and to assess whether super-resolution enhancement or red-circle visual prompting improved performance. <strong>Methods:</strong> This diagnostic accuracy study used 4746 facial images from the GestaltMatcher Database (GMDB), including 882 images in GMDB-Frequent from diseases with at least 6 images and 3864 images in GMDB-Rare from diseases with fewer than 6 images. Three locally deployed open-weight MLLMs—Gemma-4-31B, Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, and Qwen3.8-27B—generated 50 ranked candidate diagnoses under raw image, CodeFormer-enhanced, Qwen-Image-Edit-2511–generated red-circle–prompted, mismatched red-circle, and 2-stage image-to-phenotype conditions. Candidate diagnoses were mapped to Mondo Disease Ontology (MONDO) identifiers. In addition, we evaluated a random baseline, visual-prompt quality, expert-annotated regions, repeated-inference stability, model-reported confidence, diagnostic-list perplexity, prediction overlap, and disease composition. <strong>Results:</strong> Top-50 accuracy ranged from 13.95% to 18.25% in GMDB-Frequent and from 0.57% to 1.35% in GMDB-Rare. Red-circle prompting produced numerical accuracy increases in 5 of 6 model-subset comparisons. Qwen3.8-27B showed a significant improvement in GMDB-Rare, with accuracy increasing from 0.57% to 1.27% (adjusted <i>P</i>=.009). In the good visual-prompt subgroup of GMDB-Frequent, Gemma-4-31B showed significant improvements over the super-resolution condition in both physician-rated analyses, with accuracy increases of 5.76 percentage points (Holm-adjusted <i>P</i>=.005) and 4.46 percentage points (Holm-adjusted <i>P</i>=.048), respectively. Matched red-circle inputs yielded higher accuracy than mismatched inputs across all model-subset comparisons, with adjusted <i>P</i>&gt;.05. The image-to-phenotype workflow improved accuracy for Gemma-4-31B in GMDB-Frequent and Qwen3.8-27B in GMDB-Rare. In GMDB-Rare, the random baseline achieved 8.15% accuracy and exceeded all 3 models under the raw image and red-circle conditions. Prediction sets remained substantially overlapping after patient-image replacement, and top-1 predictions concentrated on a small number of diseases. <strong>Conclusions:</strong> Under the evaluated experimental settings, the 3 MLLMs showed limited ability to diagnose rare genetic diseases from facial images, particularly diseases in GMDB-Rare. Visual prompting and image-to-phenotype diagnosis produced benefits in specific models and subgroups. The prediction-overlap, mismatched-image, and disease-composition analyses indicate that model outputs were only weakly constrained by patient-specific facial information. Further studies should evaluate physician comparisons, prospective multicenter data, and phenotype-grounded multimodal diagnostic workflows.

### [Exchange of High-Frequency Medical Monitoring Data Using Fast Healthcare Interoperability Resources for Interoperable Integration Into Clinical Practice and Research: Methodological Development Study](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e94282)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-11-02` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e94282`

Background: Continuous patient monitoring generates vast volumes of high-frequency monitoring data, such as electrocardiogram, electroencephalography, and invasive blood pressure tracings, that are often persisted in heterogeneous, noninteroperable formats, limiting integration with electronic health records and secondary use. Although Fast Healthcare Interoperability Resources (FHIR) supports general health data exchange, native support for high-frequency medical monitoring data, including associated metadata and data quality metrics, remains limited. Objective: This study aims to develop a modality-agnostic FHIR package implementing a pattern to enable scalable, semantically precise representation of high-frequency medical monitoring data, decoupling structured clinical metadata from raw signal storage. Methods: This study provides the FHIR package Time Series Integration and Data in Endpoints (TIDE), a collection of FHIR Release 4 artifacts for interoperable exchange and the standardization of high-frequency monitoring clinical data, using electroencephalography as the primary use case while remaining applicable to generic medical monitoring data. The core TIDE profile separates technical metadata and analytical summaries stored directly in FHIR from high-volume raw data referenced via resources. Quantitative results are standardized with pattern slicing, including sampling frequency, statistical summaries, and data quality metrics. Semantic interoperability was achieved through a hybrid terminology binding, using Systematized Nomenclature of Medicine Clinical Terms, Logical Observation Identifiers Names and Codes for modalities, and custom for technical metadata, waveform modalities, channel identifiers, and connection types not covered by standard terminologies. All profiles were authored in FHIR Shorthand and compiled with SUSHI to produce a validated Implementation Guide conformant with the FHIR Release 4 core specification. Results: The resulting framework provides a modality-agnostic structure for high-frequency monitoring data, comprising 7 primary FHIR artifacts that define 16 distinct component slices for metadata and signal analysis results. resources reference associated and instances, encapsulate acquisition parameters, and link to full-time-series repositories. Controlled vocabularies ensure unambiguous exchange, thereby enabling downstream use in research and decision support. The architecture decouples the metadata layer from the storage layer via a standardized extension, enabling efficient querying of signals without retrieving large amounts of raw high-frequency monitoring data. Conclusions: TIDE provides a reusable, standards-compliant framework for integrating continuous high-frequency monitoring data into FHIR-based ecosystems. By separating semantically rich, queryable metadata from raw signal storage, TIDE enables longitudinal patient monitoring and automated signal quality-based triage without requiring modification of existing signal storage infrastructure.

### [Global, regional and national burden of cervical cancer in 1990–2023 with forecasts to 2050: a Global Burden of Disease study](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04729-4)

- Source: `nature_medicine` · published `2026-11-02` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04729-4`

Although highly preventable, cervical cancer remains one of the most common malignancies among women worldwide. As part of the Global Burden of Disease study, we estimated cervical cancer incidence, mortality and disability-adjusted life-years (DALYs) across 204 countries and territories from 1990 to 2023, with forecasts to 2050. In 2023, 887,000 new cases (95% uncertainty interval 682,000–1,170,000), 369,000 deaths (292,000–475,000) and 13.3 million DALYs (10.1–17.4) were estimated globally. Age-standardized incidence rate (ASIR) and age-standardized mortality rates (ASMR) were more than four and seven times higher in low- versus high-income countries. Between 1990 and 2023, global ASIR was largely stable (−0.4% (–26.6 to 34.7)), whereas ASMR changed by −17.5% (–36.2 to 13.4). All cervical cancer DALYs were attributable to human papillomavirus infection, with tobacco use contributing 5.9% (3.2–9.3) globally. By 2050, global ASIR and ASMR are forecast to change by −12.5% (–25.7 to 3.5) and −9.0% (–21.5 to 6.6) from 2024, yet absolute cases and deaths are forecast to rise by 20.2% (4.6–38.5) and 39.3% (23.6–57.3). ASIR and ASMR are forecast to remain highest in low-income countries. Intensified action is needed to accelerate progress. Analyses of the Global Burden of Disease study reported 887,000 new cases and 369,000 deaths from cervical cancer worldwide in 2023, which are forecasted to rise by 2050.
